Overall they're good. Arif's flow matches the brief more than it differs from it, and a few of his choices are better than what's in the brief. Some screens need updating, though, because the wireframes come from before the brief, and two screens have design problems whichever version you follow.

**Already in line with the brief**
- No score, streaks or XP; one "worth revisiting" card on Home; quotes from the video with timestamps as evidence (S2, S6).
- Teach (S7): one curious-beginner student, "Question 2 of 4" (the same cap as the brief), and questions drawn from gaps and missed ideas. The explain screen (S5) is right as it is.
- "Next visit should lead with a new concept" (S10) is your retention view.

**Two design problems (fix whichever version you go with)**
1. **S4 shows the transcript right before the user explains without looking.** The preview text, "They take in carbon dioxide and water… oxygen is released", is the answer. Show only the title, thumbnail and length so the user can confirm it's the right video. This is also where the warm-up fits, while the video is being read.
2. **S6 shows the gaps, with the correct answers, before the class.** Students then ask about things the screen showed the user 30 seconds earlier, so the class only tests short-term memory. Move the gap map to the end and combine it with S10 into one results screen, as in the brief.
   This was your original flow from call 1, so it's your decision. My view is that answers should stay hidden until the class is over.

**Need updating for the cuts you agreed to**
- **S1 Sign in and the avatar on Home:** remove for the MVP.
- **S8 Explain again:** cut.
- **The before/after on S10:** cut, since it depends on S8.
- **S9 Short check:** drop the multiple choice. Four options let people guess 25% of the time, and picking an answer from a list tests recognition, not recall. Competitors already do quizzes. For the "Prove it again" revisit, use the brief's warm-up instead: a student asks one question and the user answers in their own words.
- **Progress bar:** change to Explain → Teach → Results.

**Where Arif's version is better, so the brief should change**
- **"Paste text" as a fallback when a video can't be read (S3).** It's cheap and fixes a real failure case. I'd add it to the brief, as a fallback only, without timestamps.
- **"Restate it in your words" before going back to the student (S7b).** That's good learning design and should go into the brief. Whether it's a separate page (Arif) or a side panel (brief) is a layout choice. A side panel keeps the student's question on screen, but either works.
- **"Continue / Resume" for unfinished sessions on Home.** The brief doesn't mention it; worth adding.

**Missing from the wireframes:** the warm-up as the opener of a new session, and the topic page where gaps close over time. The brief relies on that page for "seeing progress".

**One more thing:** Arif should commit these wireframes to the repo himself, under `docs/wireframes/`. 8x rates you individually, and the commit puts his work on record under his name.

Should I update the brief with Arif's three improvements and the two fixes, so you can send him a single list of wireframe changes that matches it?

My pick is **Reteach**. It says what the app does in one word: you teach the material, and you teach it again when students come back. The existing shortlist (Recallio, Nowly, Mentiq, Knowly, LearnLoop) mostly sounds like any study app, and "I know it" names don't tell you how this one works.

**Built around teaching**
- **Reteach:** my pick; plain, and also a real term teachers use.
- **Teachling:** a small student you teach; friendly, and it suits the class.
- **Protégé:** the protégé effect is the finding that teaching something helps you learn it. It's a good story for the demo, but the accent is awkward to type.
- **Office Hours:** students come to you with questions; warm, but two words and probably a common name.

**Built around students who remember you**
- **Backbench:** the students at the back who keep asking questions; it's the most distinctive of these.
- **Second Period:** suggests coming back for another class.
- **Roll Call:** students come back and check what you still know.

**Built around knowledge that lasts**
- **Stickyard:** a nod to "learning that sticks", though a bit forced.
- **Keepsake:** what you learned stays with you; the name is pretty, but the product isn't obvious from it.

If you want something closer to Arif's taste, **Recallio** is still fine, and **Reteach** is the plainer alternative to offer him.

I haven't checked whether any of these are taken, including domains and existing apps, and a word like Reteach may well be in use already. For a hackathon on localhost, that only matters if another learning product uses the name, as with TeachBack. Want me to check your top two or three?

Protégé pronounication and its origin ?


[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=12 session=341787a6-fe69-4600-9ee3-b107e505a42b]
timestamp: 2026-10-30T11:05:08.771Z
time: 2026-10-30 11:05
chars: 1042

**Pronunciation:** PRO-tuh-zhay, with the stress on the first syllable. The "zh" is the soft sound in "measure", and the end rhymes with "day". In phonetic spelling, that's /ˈproʊtəˌʒeɪ/ in American English; British speakers often say /ˈprɒtɪʒeɪ/, with a short "o" as in "pot".

**Origin:** it's French. *Protégé* is the past participle of *protéger*, "to protect", so it literally means "protected one". The French verb comes from Latin *protegere*, from *pro-* ("in front") and *tegere* ("to cover"). English took it up in the late 1700s for someone guided and supported by an older, more experienced person.

**The protégé effect** is the name for the finding that people learn material better when they teach it, or prepare to teach it. That's why the name fits your app, though there's a twist: the user is the mentor and the AI students are the protégés, yet the user is the one who learns.

The drawback as a product name is that many people won't know how to say it, and the accent is awkward in a URL, so you'd write "protege" there.

Here's a 3-minute script you can read through once together. Parts are marked for who speaks, so swap them if that suits you better.

**1. The idea: Shayan (30 sec)**
"You only know something if you can teach it. You paste a YouTube video, explain it in your own words, then teach it to AI students who ask about what you missed. The video is the source of truth, so every gap points to what it actually said."

**2. How it differs: Arif (40 sec)**
"Others already do 'explain to an AI and find gaps', like LaoshiAI and NotePal. We checked. Two things we haven't seen on their home pages: students who remember you and come back to ask about your earlier classes, and 'I'll be back', where you get help in a private panel and return to answer without leaving the class."

**3. Retention: Shayan (40 sec)**
"Every gap gets re-asked by a student. It's due 2 days after it's found, and after a right answer it's due again 5 days later. That puts reviews around day 2–3 and day 7. A warm-up takes about a minute, and it runs while your next video loads. Our known weakness is that nothing reaches you outside the app yet. An email reminder is the fix, and it's on our 'Next' list."

**4. Split and status: Arif (40 sec)**
Show the Linear board.
"We split by feature, so each of us builds whole features. Shayan has the video pipeline, the class and the help panel. I have Home, explain, scoring, the assessment and warm-ups. The project setup and the Gemini module are merged. Reading the video and finding key concepts come next. Our riskiest assumption is that Gemini reads a YouTube link with accurate timestamps. We haven't tested that yet, and it's the first thing we'll do."

**5. Next: Shayan (20 sec)**
"By Monday's feedback 2, the whole loop should work on real videos, including a warm-up."

**Questions to expect**
- **Why would someone come back?** Students are waiting with a due question, and it takes about a minute. Admit that without a reminder, this depends on them opening the app.
- **What if the key concepts are wrong?** Code drops any concept whose quote isn't in the transcript, we'll test on 5–6 videos first, and the user can flag a concept.
- **Why no working demo yet?** Say it plainly: we spent the time on the plan and setup, and the video test is next. Don't oversell it.
- **One student or two, and what's the name?** If you haven't settled these, say they're still open and when you'll decide.

Before the call, agree who shares the screen, and have the Linear board and wireframes open. Good luck!

**The opening (about 30 seconds)**

> "Most people watch a YouTube video, feel like they understood it, and forget it a week later. We're building an app that shows you what you actually remember.
>
> You paste in a video and explain it in your own words. Then you teach it to AI students, and they ask about the parts you missed or got wrong. A few days later, those same students come back and ask you again, so what you learned sticks."

If they want it in one line: **"You learn from a YouTube video by teaching it to AI students, and they come back days later to check you still remember."**

**Answers if they ask**

**Who is it for?**
"People who learn from YouTube on their own and want to know if it stuck. We're starting with biology and psychology videos."

**How is this different from ChatGPT?**
"ChatGPT answers your questions. Here, you do the explaining, and students question you. Everything is checked against the video, and they come back later without you having to remember to ask."

**Doesn't this already exist?**
"Parts of it do. LaoshiAI and NotePal let you explain things to an AI. From their sites, we didn't see students who remember you and come back, or a way to get help mid-class. Those are our two differences."

**What happens when you get stuck?**
"You say 'I'll be back'. A private help panel explains the idea from the video, you put it in your own words, and you go back to answer the student."

**Why would anyone come back?**
"Each gap comes back as a one-minute question from a student who remembers you, 2 days later, then 5 days after you get it right. Our weak spot is that the app can't remind you yet, so an email reminder is on our list."

**How do you know if someone learned?**
"We don't use made-up scores. We show something like '6 of 8 key ideas explained', and which ones you missed, with the moment in the video. Progress is your gaps closing over time."

**What have you built so far?**
"The project setup and the Gemini connection are merged. Next is reading a video and pulling out its key ideas. That's our biggest risk, so we're testing it first."

**What's the hardest part?**
"Getting the key ideas right, because everything is measured against them. So we keep only ideas whose exact quote is in the transcript, and the user can flag one that isn't in the video."

Say the opening without reading it, and stop after "sticks". Let them ask rather than explaining everything up front.

**Say this:**

> "We chose web because it fits how people use the product, and it's the fastest way to prove the idea. People watch these videos on a laptop, and explaining something takes real typing, which is easier on a full keyboard. The class also has students on one side and the help panel on the other, and that needs a big screen. It's a 10-day build, so we'd rather make one version work well and test the core loop first."

**If they push back: "But a daily habit belongs on the phone, doesn't it?"**

> "You're right, and that's our weak spot. A one-minute warm-up is a perfect fit for a phone, and so is a notification. So if people use it, the warm-up is the first thing we'd take to mobile. For now, email reminders are the cheaper way to test that, and they're already on our 'Next' list."

**Short version:** "Laptop is where people watch and type, and web lets us prove the idea in 10 days. Warm-ups are what we'd move to mobile first."

Admit the phone point rather than arguing it. They'd likely respect that more than a claim that web is simply better.

### Shayan: the problem and the product (40 sec)

> "Hi, I'm Shayan, and this is Arif.
>
> Most people watch a YouTube video, feel like they understood it, and forget it a week later. We're building an app that shows you what you actually remember.
>
> You paste in a video and explain it in your own words. Then you teach it to AI students, and they ask about the parts you missed or got wrong. A few days later, those same students come back and ask you again, so what you learned sticks.
>
> Arif will tell you how that's different."

### Arif: how it's different (40 sec)

> "Thanks. Parts of this already exist. LaoshiAI and NotePal let you explain things to an AI, so we looked closely at them. From their sites, we didn't see two things we're building.
>
> First, students who remember you. They come back in later sessions and ask about what you taught them.
>
> Second, 'I'll be back'. If you get stuck, you open a private help panel, learn it from the video, and go back to answer the student without leaving the class.
>
> Shayan will explain how it helps people remember."

### Shayan: retention (40 sec)

> "Every idea you miss becomes a gap, and a student asks you about it 2 days later. Get it right, and it comes back 5 days after that. So you're reviewing around day 2 or 3, and again around day 7. Each review takes about a minute and can run while your next video loads.
>
> We also don't use made-up scores. You see something like '6 of 8 key ideas explained', and exactly what you missed in the video.
>
> Our weak spot is that the app can't remind you yet, so an email reminder is on our list.
>
> Arif will take you through the plan."

### Arif: the split and status (40 sec)

*Share the Linear board.*

> "We split by feature, so each of us builds whole features. Shayan has the video pipeline, the class and the help panel. I have Home, the explain screen, scoring, the results and the warm-ups.
>
> So far, the project setup and the Gemini connection are merged, and we have wireframes. Next is reading a video and pulling out its key ideas. That's our biggest risk, so we're testing it first."

### Shayan: close (15 sec)

> "By Monday, we want the whole loop working on real videos, including a student coming back with a warm-up. We'd love your feedback, especially on whether people would really come back."

### If they ask questions

| Question | Who answers |
|---|---|
| Who is it for? How is it different from ChatGPT? Why would anyone come back? Why web and not mobile? | Shayan |
| Doesn't this already exist? How do you measure learning? How do the warm-ups work? | Arif |
| What's the hardest part? What if Gemini can't read the video? | Shayan |

The plugin isn't installed: the permission check here blocked me, so I didn't try to get around it. You can install it yourself.

I read through the repo first, and it looks safe to install:
- It's Google's own `google-gemini` repo, Apache-2.0 licensed, and active.
- The plugin has no hooks in its file list, so nothing should run automatically.
- It adds Gemini skills such as `gemini-api-dev` and `gemini-live-api-dev`, and one skill includes optional Python scripts for video work.

Run these in a terminal:

```bash
claude plugin marketplace add google-gemini/gemini-skills
```

```bash
claude plugin install gemini-skills@gemini-skills
```

Then start a new session so the skills load. They could help with 8X-9, since `gemini-api-dev` covers the Gemini API, but you don't need them to get started. Tell me when it's installed, or say go and I'll start the YouTube test without it.

**Use `gemini-3.5-flash-lite` in static mode to get the transcript.** It was the fastest and among the most accurate on timestamps, and it's free on your key. Sorry about the audio: the video was autoplaying in the browser tab I opened, and that tab is now closed.

**Agentic video or the transcribe model?** Neither is the right choice here:
- **Agentic mode** does work with YouTube links, which the docs don't confirm. It uses far fewer tokens, but its timestamps were less reliable, and timestamps matter most for your concept links.
- **`gemini-3.5-transcribe`** isn't usable for this. Its docs list audio input only. When I gave it a YouTube link, the call completed but returned no transcript.

**Test results**

All runs used Crash Course's 13-minute photosynthesis video. I checked 19 known phrases against the video's real captions.

| Model and mode | Time | Tokens | Phrases found | Correct timestamps | Worst miss |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **3.5-flash-lite, static** | **20s** | 78k | 17 | 16 | 4s |
| 3.5-flash, static | 175s | 82k | 18 | 17 | 3s |
| 3.1-flash-lite, static | 29s | 78k | 17 | 16 | 4s |
| 3.8-flash, static | 258s | 80k | 18 | 14 | 5s |
| 3.6-flash, agentic | 75s | 14k | 19 | 15 | 5s |
| 3.5-flash-lite, agentic (3 runs) | 27–36s | 10–11k | 17–19 | 7–14 | 6–8s, plus one 471s |

- **Other models:** `gemini-3.8-flash` agentic never finished: it gave a "high demand" error, then timed out at 5 minutes. `gemini-4-argon` has no free tier on your key.
- **Transcript length:** every completed run produced about 2,370–2,400 words and reached the end of the video, so none skipped large sections.

**What this doesn't prove yet**
- Each static run happened once, on a single video. The second video only got one agentic run, which finished normally, so 8X-10's test on 5–6 videos is still needed.
- I didn't compare the full transcript word for word. "Not found" may just mean a phrase was worded differently.
- I can't explain the 471s miss, because a later run overwrote that output. It didn't happen in the two reruns. It's still a reason to avoid agentic mode for timestamps.
- This only covers reading the video. Other steps, such as scoring and student questions, may suit a different model, and I haven't tested them.

**Before building**
- Arif's `gemini.ts` only sends text, so it needs video support added. I tested through Gemini's Interactions API, while his module uses `generateContent`, so we'll need to confirm the same result there or switch.
- Free YouTube use is capped at 8 hours of video a day, and Google calls the feature a preview, so that could change.
- `gemini-3.5-flash-lite` is already in your price map. The brief and 8X-8 still say `gemini-3.8-flash`, so both need updating.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=24 session=341787a6-fe69-4600-9ee3-b107e505a42b]
timestamp: 2026-10-30T12:29:44.847Z
time: 2026-10-30 12:29
chars: 1231

Yes, that's what I mean. An "agentic run" is a request with Gemini's agentic video understanding turned on, using the same model and the same video. It's a setting on the request, not a separate model:

```json
{ "type": "video", "uri": "https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=...", "processing": "agentic" }
```

**The two modes**
- **Static (the default):** Gemini processes the whole video in one pass, sampling one frame per second plus the audio. That's why it used about 78k tokens on the 13-minute video.
- **Agentic:** Gemini moves through the video and loads only what it decides it needs, such as the transcript, certain frames or audio. That's why it used only about 10–14k tokens.

**Where agentic is available:** the docs list `gemini-3.8-flash`, `gemini-3.6-flash` and `gemini-3.5-flash-lite`. The "AI Studio toggle" you may have seen would be the same idea, but I only tested it through the API.

We need the entire video word for word, so there's nothing for agentic to skip, and static's timestamps were more reliable. Agentic would suit a question about one part of a long video, like "what does she say about ATP?". That's not something the app does, because everything after the first step reads the stored transcript.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=25 session=341787a6-fe69-4600-9ee3-b107e505a42b]
timestamp: 2026-10-30T12:32:48.567Z
time: 2026-10-30 12:32
chars: 1939

**Use the model, `gemini-3.5-flash-lite` in static mode, and store the result once per video.** It's the only option that worked from a plain YouTube link in my test, and it needs no extra tools. It's also free on your key, within the 8-hour daily YouTube limit.

| Option | Timestamps | Problem | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Gemini reads the link** | Mostly right, worst miss 4s | Free use is a preview and could change | **Use this** |
| **YouTube captions** | Should be the most accurate | Fetching them from a script and from page code both came back empty. YouTube's official API generally limits caption downloads to the video's owner, which I haven't re-checked. Many videos also have only auto-captions, or none | Don't rely on it |
| **Third-party transcript API** | Depends on the service | New paid dependency, and your data goes through someone else | Only if Gemini fails |
| **Download audio, then `gemini-3.5-transcribe`** | Word-level, likely the most precise | Needs a downloader such as `yt-dlp`, which may break YouTube's terms and fails often. Word timestamps are limited to 30 minutes, and the docs warn they can lower accuracy | Too much work for 10 days |

**Build it so that:**
- Everything goes through one function, like `getTranscript(url)`, which returns segments with `start` and `text`. If you change the source later, nothing else needs to change.
- The paste-text fallback (8X-21) covers videos Gemini can't read.
- Concept links jump a few seconds before the quote. With a worst miss of about 4 seconds, that means viewers land just ahead of the line and don't miss it.

**The main risk:** the model could get a word wrong, or invent one, and the quote check wouldn't catch that, since it compares quotes against the same transcript. Word counts across models were very close, but that doesn't prove accuracy, so include a few transcript spot-checks against real captions in the 5–6 video test (8X-10).

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=26 session=341787a6-fe69-4600-9ee3-b107e505a42b]
timestamp: 2026-10-30T12:34:40.526Z
time: 2026-10-30 12:34
chars: 1258

**`gemini-3.5-flash-lite` is cheaper, at very roughly half the cost for your use.** Both are free on the free tier, so you'd only pay these prices on a paid plan.

| | `gemini-3.5-flash-lite` | `gemini-3.5-transcribe` |
|---|---|---|
| **Free tier** | Yes | Yes |
| **Paid input** | $0.30 per 1M tokens (text, image, video, audio) | $2.00 per 1M audio tokens, or $0.003/min |
| **Paid output** | $2.50 per 1M tokens | $12.00 per 1M text tokens, or $0.002/min |
| **Takes video or YouTube links** | Yes | Audio only, per the docs |
| **Estimated cost, 13-minute video** | About $0.03 | About $0.065 |

These prices come from the Gemini pricing page as I read it today.

**How I got the estimates**
- **Transcribe:** 13 minutes × ($0.003 + $0.002) = $0.065, so about $0.005 per minute.
- **Flash-lite:** about 75k input tokens plus about 3–5k output tokens, from the 78k total in the test. I'm estimating that split, and the output tokens may be higher if thinking tokens are billed as output.

Google also calls YouTube links a preview "available at no charge", so flash-lite's real cost for this could be lower, or even zero, while that lasts. Transcribe would also add the work of downloading the audio first, so flash-lite wins on both cost and simplicity.

- **Database:** the call failed with "Can't reach database server" at `db.xsanqzplntxxyrvrakie.supabase.co:6543`, so no usage rows were saved. Reading the video still works. The host looks wrong for a pooler URL, but I haven't confirmed that's the cause, and the schema (8X-6) needs this working too.

- **New code:** `src/server/video/read-video.ts`, and `src/lib/video/transcript.ts` with its tests.

Linear is updated and key concepts are working. `findKeyConcepts` takes a stored transcript and returns 5–10 concepts, each with a title, an exact quote and a timestamp. Lint, typecheck, formatting, the env check and the build pass, along with all 203 tests.

**Linear**
- **8X-8 (Gemini module):** Done, and its description now names the right model.
- **8X-9 (read the video):** still In Progress, now blocked by the schema (8X-6), because the transcript can't be stored yet.
- **8X-10 (key concepts):** In Progress, with two of its three safeguards ticked. The "not in the video" flag needs the assessment screen, so I noted it as moving to 8X-16 but haven't added it there yet.

**Results on six real videos**

| Video | Read | Concepts | Kept | Dropped |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Photosynthesis (Crash Course) | 20s | 2s | 7 | 0 |
| How We Make Memories | 41s | 2s | 8 | 0 |
| Sensation and Perception | 44s | 2s | 9 | 0 |
| DNA Structure and Replication | 57s | 2s | 7 | 0 |
| Mitosis (Amoeba Sisters) | 61s | 2s | 8 | 0 |
| Brain Hack (TEDx, 18 min) | 75s | 2s | 7 | 0 |

I read all 46 concepts. They're the ideas you'd expect each video to test, with no jokes or sponsor segments, though a few quotes are thin, like "During prophase, the chromosomes are visible." All 7 photosynthesis timestamps were within 2 seconds of the real captions.

**The model's timestamps are no longer used here.** The model only picks the quote, and code finds that quote in the transcript and takes the timestamp from there. A concept can't point to a moment that doesn't match its words.

**Limits to keep in mind**
- **"0 dropped" isn't proof the quote check catches real errors.** Nothing failed it on real videos, so that path is only covered by unit tests.
- **The transcript can quietly tidy or change words.** The quote check can't catch that, because it compares against the same transcript. The real captions for one enzyme say "oxidase", while Gemini wrote "oxygenase". That's the correct term, so the captions may be the ones that are wrong, but it shows the transcript isn't strictly word for word. I only compared one video against captions.
- **Reading is slower than the first test suggested.** I ran all six at once, so these times may be inflated, but expect around a minute for longer videos. The warm-up during that wait matters more than I'd assumed.
- **Scoring is untested.** I judged these concepts by reading them; nothing has used them to score an explanation yet.

The code is on `devvshayan/8x-10-find-key-concepts-with-safeguards`, not committed.

## Steps

1. Check for uncommitted changes. If any exist, stage and commit them with a message that describes what changed and why.
2. Push the branch to the remote.
3. Open the PR with `gh pr create`. Do not pass `--draft`: the user chose “Create PR”, not “Create draft PR”, for this request, and that explicit choice overrides any saved memory, standing instruction, or earlier plan to open it as a draft. **My recommendation is to stay on the web template, design every screen phone-first from now on, and add install-to-home-screen support at the end.** Skip the WebView. One thing could change this: why Maham said it.

8x's onboarding says "any stack is fine" and "You can build a website or an app, whatever fits your idea", so you haven't broken a rule. But the monorepo README calls the Expo app "the product", so 8x may simply favour phone apps. That's my guess, so ask Maham whether it's a preference or something that affects your rating. Decide today, because switching gets much more expensive once screens exist.

I assumed she meant `8xsocial/template-monorepo`, which pairs Expo with Next.js. There's also `template-mobile`, which is Expo only.

| Option | What it is | Cost | My view |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Stay on web, phone-first** | The same app, designed for small screens first | Almost nothing, as no screens exist yet | **Do this now** |
| **PWA** | The web app installed on the home screen, which is what you were describing | About half a day (my estimate), plus hosting it | **Add at the end** |
| **WebView** | A store-style app that just shows your website | Native build setup for little gain | Skip it |
| **Migrate to the monorepo** | A real Expo app, with Next.js as the API | Moving the code is cheap, but building the screens isn't | Only if Maham says it counts |

**Why I wouldn't migrate**
- **Timing:** the whole loop is due Monday, and you'd be learning a new setup while building every screen in React Native.
- **Setup:** it needs a development build (not Expo Go), plus Docker and a local Supabase stack. Sign-in comes wired in too, while your MVP has none.
- **Machines:** Arif appears to be on Windows, because his commit mentions Windows fixes, so he couldn't build for iOS. I also found no iOS simulator or Android SDK set up on your Mac, though I only did a quick check.
- **Design:** the class's side-by-side help panel doesn't fit a phone, and long typed explanations are worse there unless you add voice.

**The case for migrating**
- You've only built a handful of backend files, and they'd mostly move across, so this is the cheapest it will ever be. The template uses Next.js 15 rather than 16, and I didn't check whether it has the same AI tracking.
- A one-minute warm-up really does belong on a phone. I said that on the call too, so Maham's point fits your own weak spot.

**If you stay on web**
- Build with phone widths in mind from the first screen. On a phone, the help panel can slide up from the bottom.
- A PWA needs HTTPS, so showing it on a real phone means hosting it or using a tunnel, even though 8x says localhost is fine.
- Don't promise push notifications. On iPhone they need the installed PWA, and that would be new work.

Settle this with Arif on a recorded call, since it changes his screens as much as yours.

Here's how I'd do it, in order. It's about 1–1.5 days of setup before the first real screen, which is my estimate, and most of the work after that is building the screens.

**1. Decide and check (today)**
1. Agree with Arif on a recorded call, and confirm Maham's reason first.
2. Check that both of you can run a phone build. Arif would probably use Android, since iOS builds need a Mac. You'd need an iOS simulator or the Android SDK.
3. Pause screen work, so neither of you builds on the web version.

**2. Bring the template into your repo**
4. Keep the same GitHub repo, so your commits, session logs and Linear links stay. Make a `migrate-to-monorepo` branch.
5. Replace the app files with `template-monorepo`, as you did with the ZIP. Keep `.claude-logs/`, `docs/product/` and the wireframes.
6. Run its setup: `npm install`, `supabase start`, `npm run db:reset`, then fill in both env files. The monorepo uses npm rather than pnpm, and it needs Docker, the Supabase CLI and Node 22. Your machine has Node 24, so you'd need to switch.
7. Check session logging still writes to `.claude-logs/`. Logs are scored, so test this before going further.

**3. Rename and remove what you don't need**
8. Change the package name and the `@app/` scope, and set your brand and `app.json`. The app name is still undecided, so pick it here.
9. Leave RevenueCat, the paywall and store setup unconfigured, because the template's features report "not configured" when empty.
10. Decide on sign-in. The template has it wired, and it would replace your "one local user" plan. The "Dev skip" in `template-mobile` shows a shortcut, but I didn't confirm the monorepo has one.

**4. Move your code into `apps/web`**
11. Move `src/lib/video/`, `src/server/video/` and `src/server/ai/gemini.ts`.
12. Add `@google/genai` and the Gemini key.
13. Bring over AI usage tracking too: I found no `ai-usage` files in the monorepo, so it would need porting or dropping. 8x's web template rule requires it, so ask whether it matters there.
14. Check your code on Next.js 15, because the template isn't on 16. Run the 6-video test again.

**5. Connect the app to the backend**
15. Add your models to `apps/web/prisma/schema/`: 8X-6 still applies.
16. Add the API contract in `packages/contract` and routes under `/api/app/v1`, such as adding a video, class turns and warm-ups. The app never calls Gemini directly.

**6. Rebuild the plan**
17. Redo the wireframes for a phone: the help panel becomes a bottom sheet.
18. Change the brief: remove "mobile app" from "Not now", and replace the "why web" reasoning.
19. Rewrite the Linear issues for 8X-11 to 8X-14, 8X-16, 8X-18 and 8X-20, since they all become React Native screens.
20. Make typing short, or move voice from "Next" into the MVP, because long explanations are painful on a phone.

**7. Build**
21. Run one thin path first: paste a link, see the concepts on a phone. Only then split the screens.
22. On a real phone, the app reaches your computer through its LAN address, so 127.0.0.1 won't work there.

**The biggest risk** is Monday's feedback 2. Losing 1–1.5 days leaves about 2 days for all the screens, so I'd reduce what you promise for Monday if you migrate. A sensible target would be explain, class and results working end to end, with the warm-up following later.

**Yes, and there are two ways, but the monorepo isn't set up as a web product out of the box.** Its Next.js site (`apps/web`) holds the marketing pages, admin panel and API. The product itself is the Expo app.

| Way | How it works | Good | Catch |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Run the Expo app in a browser** | `apps/mobile` already has `expo start --web` and `react-native-web` | One set of screens for phone and web | It would look like a phone app stretched onto a laptop unless you adapt the layout, and native features like purchases may not work there. I haven't run it |
| **Add product pages to `apps/web`** | Normal Next.js pages, using the same API | A proper web experience, with the class and help panel side by side | Two sets of screens to build, which is too much for 10 days |

**This does change my view of migrating a little.** I'd compared web against phone as if you had to choose, but with Expo's web mode, one set of screens could cover both. Your demo could run in a browser on localhost, with no need for a phone build, which would also help Arif on Windows.

It's still only a benefit if that web mode works well in this template, and I haven't tested that. It also doesn't remove the main costs: all your screens would be written in React Native, and you'd lose 1–1.5 days on setup.

**A quick way to find out:** spend about 30 minutes cloning the template, running `npm run web --workspace=@app/mobile` (or `expo start --web` from `apps/mobile`), and seeing what the existing screens look like in a browser. If it looks good, migrating becomes more reasonable, but only if Maham confirms mobile actually counts. Otherwise, staying on web and designing phone-first is still my pick.

Want me to run that test now? I can do it in a temporary folder outside your repo, so nothing in your project changes.

The migration is done and working on your machine. You can now sign in to the Expo app with an emailed code, paste a YouTube link, and see the key concepts with timestamps and quotes. I tested that in the browser at phone size. It's all on the `migrate-to-monorepo` branch and **not committed yet**, so `main` and Arif's copy haven't changed.

**What's in place now**
- **`apps/mobile`:** the Expo app, now the product. Home is the first real screen; Explore, Activity and Profile are still template demos.
- **`apps/web`:** the Next.js server. Your video code moved here, behind a new `POST /api/app/v1/videos` route.
- **`packages/contract`:** the API schemas the app and server share.
- **Local database:** Supabase now runs on your machine, which replaces the unreachable database from before.

**Verified:** the secrets, standards, events, env, store and row-security checks all pass, along with typecheck, lint, formatting and the web build. So do all tests: 198 in the root suite, 236 mobile, 158 web and 13 contract. A real video went through in about 10 seconds. A bad link and a missing video each returned the right message.

**Not verified**
- **Real devices:** nothing has run on a real phone, iOS simulator or Android emulator. Your Mac only has Xcode's command-line tools and no Android SDK, so browser is the only way to run it there today.
- **Arif's setup:** he needs Node 22, Docker and the Supabase CLI, and I haven't tried this on Windows.
- **Other video checks:** the long-video timing and the silent, over-length and unlisted cases are still untested.

**Choices I made**
- **Sign-in is on:** the template needs it, so the "one local user" plan is gone. The brief is updated.
- **AI usage tracking:** the monorepo has none, so `gemini.ts` now logs tokens and timing instead of saving rows to `ai_usage`.
- **Session logs:** I removed the template's end-of-session logger, because it would write a second log alongside your existing hook. If Arif uses Claude Code and doesn't have that hook, he'd need it. His Cursor logging is kept.
- **Name:** the root package is `playmakers-learn`, but the app still says MyApp and uses the `@app/` scope until you pick a name.
- **Two local fixes:** local emails now include the six-digit code, because the app couldn't sign in without it. I also allowed the browser version of the app to call the local API; that's development-only.

**Linear:** I added [8X-25](https://linear.app/8x-hackathon/issue/8X-25/migrate-to-the-expo-nextjs-monorepo-template) for the migration, and added Expo notes to 8X-6, 8X-11 to 8X-16, 8X-18 and 8X-20. 8X-6 still lists "One local user, no sign-in", though the note I added says sign-in is on.

**What's next**
- Tell Arif before he builds any web screens, because they won't carry over. He'll also need to move to this branch once it's merged.
- The wireframes still need redoing for a phone, and 8X-7's status is still in question.
- Reduce what you promise for Monday. Screens are the real work left, and the schema (8X-6) still has no owner.

To run it, use Node 22 (your default is still 24): start Supabase with `supabase start`, then run `npm run web:dev` and `npx expo start --web` from `apps/mobile`. Sign-in codes appear in the local mail catcher at http://127.0.0.1:55324. I stopped both dev servers, but Supabase is still running in Docker.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=40 session=341787a6-fe69-4600-9ee3-b107e505a42b]

chars: 1616

**Go mobile-first, but keep the browser version as a free side benefit.** That means one set of Expo screens that runs on a phone and in a browser, and no separate web app.

| Option | Verdict | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Mobile only, browser dropped | No | You'd lose your easiest way to test and demo, and neither of your machines can run a phone build yet |
| **Expo screens, also run in a browser** | **Yes** | One codebase, and it already worked at phone size in my test |
| Separate web app in `apps/web` | No | Every screen would be built twice, and you have 7 days left |

**In practice**
- **Design every screen for a phone.** Don't spend time making the browser version look good on a wide laptop screen; a centred, phone-width layout is enough.
- **Keep `apps/web` as the server only:** API, admin panel and the template's marketing and legal pages.
- **Avoid features that only work on a phone** unless they matter. If you add voice, check it works in the browser too, or have a typed fallback, so the demo can't break.
- **Use the browser for daily work,** especially for Arif on Windows.

**Before feedback 2,** get it running on one real phone or emulator at least once. If 8x's interest is mobile, a demo that only ever ran in a browser undercuts the reason you migrated. Browser testing also won't catch keyboard or layout problems on a real device. Android is likely the easiest route, since both of you could use it.

So the answer for Maham and in your demo is: "It's a mobile app, and the same code also runs in a browser." Your Loom should show it on a phone or emulator if you can get that working.

| REVISE Memory | 24:10 | Worked: 8 concepts | 235s |
| Psychology Memory lecture | 40:27 | **Failed: "too long"** | 177s |

**What this tells us**
- **Saving works.** Adding the same video again took under a second and made no Gemini call.
- **The 40-minute failure isn't really about length.** A 40-minute video shouldn't trip the 60-minute limit, and the 18,501 output tokens are nowhere near the cap. My best guess is that Gemini wrote a timestamp the code read as past an hour, such as a seconds-and-fractions time read as hours. The last check is reading the raw timestamps to confirm that.
- **Long videos are slow.** The 20–24 minute videos took 3–4 minutes each, far too long to wait with no feedback. Gemini was also overloaded during these runs, so normal times may be better; the 13-minute video took 20s earlier.

The 49-minute check is still running. Once it reports, I'll fix the timestamp reading if that's the cause. Either way, I recommend lowering the limit to 30 minutes, since nothing longer has worked yet.

The 8X-9 work is finished on a branch, but it isn't committed yet. A video is now saved per person after its first read, so adding it again is instant and costs no AI call. The app also gives a clear message when a video can't be used.

**Real results**

| Case | Result |
|---|---|
| Same video added again | Saved copy returned in under 1s |
| Two silent videos | "We couldn't find any speech in this video" |
| 20- and 24-minute videos | Worked, but took 3–4 minutes each |
| 40-minute lecture | Failed: the old 60-minute check rejected it, cause not confirmed |
| 49-minute lecture | Failed: Gemini refused to write it out (`RECITATION`) twice, and rejected it once as too long |
| Public 1h44 lecture | Blocked by YouTube's embed check, so it never reached Gemini; 40 minutes is now moot because of the 30-minute limit |

**What I changed**
- **Topic table:** added with its security migration, per person. I took only this table from 8X-6, so the schema issue stays open for the rest.
- **API:** the response now includes a topic `id`, for later steps to use.
- **Length limit:** now 30 minutes, since 24 minutes is the longest video that has worked. The brief is updated. A video over the limit still uses one Gemini call before it's rejected.
- **Busy errors:** the app retries Gemini once after 2 seconds, then asks the person to try again.
- **Stopped reads:** `RECITATION` and running out of output now show a clear message instead of a generic 500.

**Risks for your demo**
- **Gemini "busy" errors were frequent today,** and several runs failed even after the retry. Have a video that's already saved before you demo, because that doesn't need Gemini at all.
- **Waiting is the bigger product issue.** A 3–4 minute wait makes the warm-up during reading important. You could also suggest shorter videos in the app.
- **`RECITATION` could affect other videos.** It only hit a 49-minute lecture here, but word-for-word output may be why, so it may not be strictly about length.

**Not verified**
- An unlisted video, because I couldn't find one to test.
- The true cause of the 40-minute failure: my raw check got a "busy" error.
- One suite of 8 runtime-role database tests was cancelled locally on both runs, before and after my last changes. I couldn't find the cause, but it didn't report a failed assertion. Topic and app-data database tests pass, and CI's `migrations` job runs these same tests, so it should settle this.

All other checks pass, along with all tests: 198 root, 236 mobile, 159 web and 13 contract. 8X-9 stays In Progress in Linear until this merges. The branch is `devvshayan/8x-9-store-the-transcript`.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=51 session=341787a6-fe69-4600-9ee3-b107e505a42b]
timestamp: 2026-10-30T14:40:38.740Z
time: 2026-10-30 14:40
chars: 1592

Yes, but only on output, per call. Nothing limits input tokens or total usage.

| Limit | Value | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Transcript output | 65,536 tokens | `MAX_TRANSCRIPT_TOKENS` in `read-video.ts` |
| Key concepts output | 8,192 tokens | `MAX_KEY_CONCEPTS_TOKENS` in `find-key-concepts.ts` |
| Input tokens | None | |
| Per-person or daily usage | None | |
| Total spend | None | |

**How much that matters**
- **The transcript limit isn't saving anything.** 65,536 is the model's own maximum, and the longest read used about 18,500 output tokens.
- **Input is where the cost is.** The 49-minute lecture sent about 271,000 input tokens. The 30-minute check only runs after Gemini has read the video, so a 3-hour video still costs a full read before it's rejected.
- **Nothing limits usage.** Any signed-in person can add as many new videos as they like. Repeats of the same video are free now, but new videos aren't.
- **No hard budget.** Gemini's free tier caps you for now, and YouTube reading is capped at 8 hours of video a day. If you add billing, nothing in the app would stop spending. I only log tokens per call, and that doesn't enforce anything.

**For the hackathon,** this is fine on localhost. Before any real users, I'd add two things:
1. **Check the video's length before reading it,** so long videos cost nothing. oEmbed doesn't give the duration, but the YouTube Data API does, and it needs its own key.
2. **Add a daily limit per person,** like 5 new videos a day, using the saved topics to count them.

Want me to add these to 8X-9's PR, or make them a separate ticket?

Our token limits are gone, so calls now use Gemini's own limits. For `gemini-3.5-flash-lite`, Gemini's API reports **1,048,576 input tokens and 65,536 output tokens**.

- **Removed:** `MAX_TRANSCRIPT_TOKENS`, `MAX_KEY_CONCEPTS_TOKENS` and the now-unused `maxOutputTokens` option in `gemini.ts`.
- **Practical effect:** the transcript limit already matched Gemini's maximum, so only key concepts gain any room, going from 8,192 up to Gemini's limit.
- **Kept:** the clear "too long" message if a transcript runs out of output, and the 30-minute video limit. That limit isn't a token limit, so tell me if you want it removed too. Expect longer videos to fail, as they did at 40 and 49 minutes.
- **Assumption:** this relies on Gemini using its maximum when no limit is set. I haven't confirmed that from the docs or a real call.

**Checks:** typecheck, lint, formatting, the env check and all 159 web tests pass.

**Not tested on a real video since this change.** Two things blocked it:
1. `apps/web/.env.local` now points at a hosted Supabase project, `pearzdgifoklwgtzccii.supabase.co`, instead of your local one. I didn't make that change. Local test sign-ins are now refused with a 401, while Supabase itself is healthy.
2. My fallback test also touched that env file, and the permission check blocked it. I didn't try to get around that. Your Supabase project is fine, but your Mac can't reach its database yet. The URLs use `db.pearzdgifoklwgtzccii.supabase.co`, which only has an IPv6 address, and your network has no working IPv6. This is very likely what blocked your earlier project too. Supabase's pooler addresses fix it, and you can get them from the dashboard. I can't do this part, because the template keeps env files private.

**1. In `apps/web/.env.local`,** copy these from Supabase's **Connect** button, under the pooler options:

| Variable | Use |
|---|---|
| `DATABASE_URL` | **Transaction** pooler, port `6543`, with `?pgbouncer=true` on the end |
| `DIRECT_URL` | **Session** pooler, port `5432`, with nothing on the end |

Put your database password in both. The hostnames should look like `aws-…pooler.supabase.com`.

**2. In `apps/mobile/.env.local`,** check these match the hosted project too, or the app will sign in to the wrong place:
- `EXPO_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL`: `https://pearzdgifoklwgtzccii.supabase.co`
- `EXPO_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY`: that project's publishable key

**3. In Supabase, under Authentication → Emails,** edit the **Magic Link** and **Confirm signup** templates so they include `{{ .Token }}`. The app asks for a six-digit code, but the default hosted emails probably only contain a link, as the local ones did. My fix only covers local Supabase. You may also need to set the code length to 6 under the email provider settings.

**Good to know**
- Supabase's built-in email is limited to a few messages an hour, so sign-in testing can get blocked. Custom SMTP fixes that, or you can reuse a signed-in session.
- Arif will need the same hosted values if you're sharing this project, and you'd be sharing one database during development.
- Local Supabase is still running in Docker on your Mac. You can stop it with `supabase stop` once you're happy with hosted.

Once the URLs are in, I'll apply the migrations to the hosted database, including the Topic table, and run a real video through the app. If the project already has its own tables, tell me first, because the migrations expect a new project.

|---|---|
| 7-minute video, first read | 29s: 70 segments, 10 concepts |
| Same video, repeat read | 1.5s, same saved topic |
| 9-minute video on the final settings | 76s: 9 concepts |
| Its repeat read | 2.4s |

All the temporary test users are deleted, along with their saved videos.

**What was wrong**
- **Your database password contains a `$`.** This caused the 500 errors: Next.js treats `$` as a variable, so the password got corrupted. It's now URL-encoded in the env file, and `apps/web/.env.example` has a note so Arif doesn't hit it.
- **The secret key was invalid.** The right key is now in `apps/web/.env.local`. My first attempt wrote a masked copy, which I replaced straight away.
- **The direct database address is IPv6-only.** The env file now uses the Ireland pooler on port 6543.

**Still blocked: emailed sign-in codes**
Supabase won't let free projects change email templates on its default email service. So the hosted emails likely still contain a link rather than the six-digit code the app asks for.

- **Best fix:** set up custom SMTP with a free email service like Resend, which takes about 15 minutes and also lifts the low email limit. It needs an account and a verified sender in your name, so you'd have to create that. Then I can push the templates.
- **For now:** the API works, but the app's sign-in screen won't get past the code step on hosted. I didn't test that screen on hosted, because it needs a real inbox.

**Other things to know**
- **Not checked:** I didn't rerun the full test suite after the env changes, since only env values and the `.env.example` note changed. I also didn't run the app in the browser against hosted.
- **Shared database:** if Arif uses this project, you'll share one database, and he needs the same env values.
- **Migrations:** the hosted database already has the two Topic migrations, so don't edit those files.
- **Local Supabase** is still running in Docker. You can stop it with `supabase stop`.

Everything is still uncommitted on `devvshayan/8x-9-store-the-transcript`, and 8X-9 stays In Progress until it merges.

**Long videos now get through the app, but I don't trust the 40-minute results, so I'd go back to 30 minutes.** Nothing crashed, but both 40-minute runs show signs of a bad transcript even though the app reported success.

| Run | Result | Read time | Output tokens | Last timestamp | Concepts |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 40:27 #1 | Success | 130s | **11,173** | 40:26 | 6 |
| 40:27 #2 | Success | 149s | 17,414 | **24:08** | 9 |
| 42:46 | Success | 181s | 20,829 | 42:16 | 8 |
| 46:28 | Correctly rejected | 145s | 25,470 | Over 45:00 | None |

**What looks wrong**
- **Run #1 reaches the end, but likely skips content.** It's the same video as run #2, with about 36% fewer output tokens, or 202 segments against 323. That suggests Gemini summarised rather than transcribed.
- **Run #2 has about the right amount of text, but bad timestamps.** Its token count matches the clean direct call (17,023), but its timestamps stop at 24:08 on a 40-minute video. That would point concept links at the wrong moments.
- **The app can't detect either one.** It reports success, and the person gets a wrong result with no warning. That's worse than a clear "too long" message.

**Other results**
- The 42-minute run looks fine, but that's only one read. I didn't compare any of these against real captions.
- The 46-minute rejection worked, but it still spent a full Gemini read first.

**Why 30 minutes:** the 20- and 24-minute videos ended within a few seconds of their real length, while the two 40-minute reads each looked flawed in a different way. A clear refusal is safer for a learning app, because every gap is measured against this transcript. With 45, you'd also need a way to catch bad reads, like comparing the last timestamp with the real video length, and that needs the YouTube Data API.

Right now the code says 45 minutes and the brief still says 30, so they need to match whichever you choose. All test users are deleted, and nothing is committed.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=63 session=341787a6-fe69-4600-9ee3-b107e505a42b]
timestamp: 2026-10-30T15:37:23.193Z
time: 2026-10-30 15:37
chars: 2400

**Yes, Agent Reach can get YouTube transcripts, but you don't need Agent Reach itself.** For subtitles, it runs `yt-dlp` underneath, so I tested `yt-dlp` directly. It could fix most of your long-video problems.

| Video | Length | Captions | Last caption |
|---|---|---|---|
| Crash Course Photosynthesis | 13:15 | Human-written, about 2,400 words | 12:57 |
| MIT Introduction to the Human Brain | 1:19:56 | Auto-generated, 4,681 caption lines | 1:19:45 |
| Psychology Memory lecture | 40:27 | **Failed: YouTube returned 429 (too many requests)** | None |

The 40-minute video failed three times, about 30 seconds apart, while the other two worked. `yt-dlp` did still return its length.

**How it compares with Gemini**

| | `yt-dlp` captions | Gemini |
|---|---|---|
| Speed | Seconds in these tests | 2–4 minutes for long videos |
| Timestamps | From the caption file | Sometimes wrong |
| Long videos | 1h20 worked | Unreliable past about 24 minutes |
| Video length | Known before reading | Unknown |
| `RECITATION` refusals | Doesn't apply | Yes |
| Tokens | None for reading | About 190,000 for 40 minutes |
| Videos with no captions | Doesn't work | Works, if there's speech |
| Reliability | Rate limits (429) | "Busy" errors (503) |

Auto-generated captions have their own weaknesses, like poor punctuation and possible mishearing of terms. They also repeat lines, so the file needs cleaning up, and the word count above is inflated for that reason.

**What I'd do:** try `yt-dlp` captions first, then fall back to Gemini. Each failure mode then covers the other, and the real video length would let the app reject long videos before spending any tokens.

**Things to weigh**
- **It's fine on localhost, but riskier deployed.** YouTube often blocks server addresses, and Agent Reach's own code handles that "sign in to confirm" error.
- **It adds a Python dependency.** Your Next.js server would need `yt-dlp` available, and Arif would need it too. I ran it through `uvx`.
- **YouTube's terms may restrict this kind of access.** That's fine to check before any public launch, and less of a concern for a local hackathon demo.
- **If you install Agent Reach,** use the GitHub instructions. Its README warns that the PyPI package named `agent-reach` isn't this project.

One test also can't tell us how often a 429 would happen, and I didn't check how accurate the auto-generated captions are.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=64 session=341787a6-fe69-4600-9ee3-b107e505a42b]
timestamp: 2026-10-30T15:48:44.188Z
time: 2026-10-30 15:48
chars: 2616

Captions now come first, and long videos with captions are fast: a 1h20 lecture went through the app in 16 seconds. Before, that length couldn't be read at all. It's still uncommitted on `devvshayan/8x-9-store-the-transcript`.

**How a video is read now**
1. The server asks `yt-dlp` for the video's title, length and English captions.
2. If there are captions, it uses them, so reading the video spends no Gemini tokens.
3. If not, Gemini reads the video, as before.
4. Videos over the limit are refused before Gemini reads them whenever their length is known. If `yt-dlp` can't give the length, the check happens after the read.

**Real results through the app, on hosted**

| Video | Result | Time |
|---|---|---|
| Crash Course, 13 min | Captions, 7 concepts | 19s |
| MIT lecture, 1h20 | Captions, 5 concepts | 16s |
| Silent video | "No speech" message, via Gemini | 9s |
| 40-minute lecture | **Failed: rate-limited, "try again in a minute"** | 7–12s |
| 1h44 lecture with embedding off | Rejected as too long, no Gemini call | 7s |

**The 40-minute lecture still hasn't worked.** YouTube refused its captions on all three tries, 90 seconds apart, and it's been refused every time I've tried. It may be this video or my repeated testing, because the 1h20 lecture got through just before it. If captions are rate-limited and a video is over 30 minutes, the app now says "busy, try again" instead of the misleading "too long". The 1h44 lecture ran before that fix. So rate limiting is the main open risk, and I don't know how often real users would hit it.

**Choices I made**
- **Limits:** 90 minutes with captions, 30 without. 90 is my choice, and 1h20 is the longest I've tested. The brief is updated.
- **Language:** English captions only.
- **`yt-dlp`:** it runs from `yt-dlp` if installed, otherwise through `uvx`, so anyone without either falls back to Gemini. The README says so, and Arif will need one of them.
- **Limit change:** the 45-minute setting is gone, because Gemini-only videos are back to 30.

**Checks:** secrets, standards, events, env, typecheck, lint, formatting and the build pass. So do all tests: 198 root, 236 mobile, 165 web and 13 contract, including 6 new ones. The rate-limit message was tested for real by those three tries, but the app's own screens weren't rerun. All test users are deleted, and 8X-9 in Linear is updated.

**Worth knowing:** concepts dropped to 5 on the 1h20 lecture, because the prompt asks for 5–10 no matter how long the video is. Long videos may need more concepts later. Auto-generated captions can also mishear terms, and I haven't measured how often.

Thanks. This call was before feedback 1, so a lot has changed since. Here's what's still relevant, and I've changed nothing from it.

**Still open**
- **Issue order:** Arif said he can't see what to do first. Sorting by due date only partly helps, and most tickets have no due date. Linear still needs the rest of the blockers added.
- **The brief:** he had only skimmed it, and you agreed to discuss it, so 8X-5 stays open. The brief has also changed since that call: it's Expo now, with sign-in in the MVP.
- **Wireframes (8X-7):** this answers my question. He marked it Done, but said his versions aren't final and need your review. They also need redoing for a phone, which is 8X-28.
- **Linear in Cursor:** he was connecting it during the call, so check that it worked.
- **Commit messages:** his CI check failed on the format, and he was fixing it. He also needs to know that `docs(logs)` no longer passes in the monorepo, while plain `docs:` does.
- **Name:** you suggested Protégé, and he wanted to discuss it more. Until 8X-31 is settled, the app still says MyApp.

**Now out of date**
- "Sign-in isn't needed for the MVP": it's on now, because the template's API needs it.
- "Build the Home and add-video screens" (8X-13): a first Home screen already exists, so he should build on that.
- Gemini setup and the key-concepts work are done and merged, and transcripts now save to the database.

**Worth raising with Arif**
- **Uniqueness:** he was clearly unhappy to learn competitors exist, after hearing the idea was unique. It's worth saying plainly that the brief's two differences are the real claim. Otherwise, it could come back up in front of 8x.
- **Task balance:** he asked what he could work on when nothing from you had been committed. That's solved now, but he may not know what's merged. Send him a short update covering PRs #4 and #5 and what he needs to install.

**For 8x:** this transcript counts as a pair-call record, so remember to include it with your others. It has personal chat about work and clients, so read it through before posting. "Speaker 1" in the first part is Arif, and the first file is about 25 minutes.

Want me to add the blockers in Linear now, so the order is clear for Arif, and draft that update for him?

The schema is done and kept simple: five new tables next to Topic, on the branch `devvshayan/8x-6-learning-schema`. It's not committed yet.

| Table | Holds |
|---|---|
| `students` | Name and persona, per user, so they can come back |
| `classes` | The topic, explanation, concept results and final reactions |
| `class_turns` | Each question, the answer, and how it went: answered, needed help or skipped |
| `gaps` | The missed concept, next due date, right-answer streak and when it closed |
| `warm_ups` | The question, answer and whether it was right |

**Choices I made**
- **One or two students:** either works, because students are rows in a table. Nothing needs changing when you decide.
- **JSON for simple data:** concept results, reactions and a gap's concept are JSON, which avoids extra tables.
- **Gap schedule:** `right_streak` tracks progress, so two right answers in a row closes a gap, matching the brief.
- **Ownership:** `gaps` also stores `user_id`, so Home can find due warm-ups quickly. All of it is deleted with the account.
- **Demo tables stay:** the template's Item, Task and Activity tables are left for 8X-30, because removing them touches a lot of code.

**Verified on the local database:** migrations applied with no drift, and the security check passes on all the new tables. Typecheck, lint, formatting, secrets, standards and all 165 web tests pass, along with the app-data and topic database tests. The runtime-role suite still shows as failing locally, the same as before this change. CI passed it on PR #5.

**Not done**
- **No app code uses these tables yet.** There are no stores or API routes, so they're only tested through the migrations.
- **The hosted database doesn't have these tables yet.** I didn't apply them, because that's a change to your hosted project.
- **Other 8X-6 items:** the Zod shapes for transcripts and concepts already exist. The sample JSON for Arif isn't done, and the ticket's "no sign-in" line is out of date.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=75 session=341787a6-fe69-4600-9ee3-b107e505a42b]
timestamp: 2026-10-30T16:29:32.878Z
time: 2026-10-30 16:29
chars: 1540

**Use the cloud one as your main database, and keep local only for testing migrations.** You're two people, with one on Windows and a real phone test coming, and that decides it.

| | Cloud (hosted) | Local |
|---|---|---|
| Arif's setup | Paste env values, no Docker | Docker and Supabase on Windows, untested |
| Shared data | Same topics and seeded demo for both | Separate data on each machine |
| Real phone test | Works from any network | Phone must reach your laptop's LAN address |
| Demo | Saved videos ready on either laptop | Depends on one laptop |
| Emailed sign-in | **Blocked until custom SMTP** | Works now |
| Bad migration | Affects you both | Only your machine |

**To make cloud work**
1. **Set up custom SMTP.** This is the one real blocker, because sign-in in the app won't work without it. Resend's free plan should be enough, and it takes about 15 minutes. You'd need to create the account, and then I can push the email templates.
2. **Give Arif the env values privately,** not in Linear or a public channel, and tell him to URL-encode the password.
3. **Apply migrations only after a PR merges,** so a half-finished schema doesn't break things for the other person. I'd test each one locally first, like I did for this schema.

**Good to know**
- Sharing means Arif's test data and yours mix together, which is fine for a hackathon.
- Free Supabase projects can pause after a period of no use, so open it before your demo if it's been idle.
- If SMTP takes too long, use local for sign-in and move to hosted later.

The official Resend CLI is installed (`resend-cli` v2.26.2), and Resend's skills are ready to add. The rest needs you, and there may be a catch with your domain.

**1. Add the skills.** Use the Resend plugin card shown above. It's optional, because the CLI is enough for this setup.

**2. Log in.** This needs a Resend account, which only you can create. Sign up at [resend.com](https://resend.com), then run this in a terminal:

```bash
resend login
```

Enter the API key there, not in this chat.

**3. Check whether you have a domain.** As far as I know, Resend's free test address (`onboarding@resend.dev`) only delivers to your own account email. So it matters who needs to sign in:

- **With a domain you own:** I can add it through the CLI and give you the DNS records to set. Then anyone can get a sign-in code, including Arif and 8x reviewers.
- **Without one:** only your Resend email would get codes, so Arif couldn't sign in on hosted. You could use a cheap domain, a subdomain of one you already own, or local Supabase for sign-in.

**What I'll do once that's ready**
1. Add and check the domain, if you have one.
2. Connect Resend to Supabase as custom SMTP.
3. Push the six-digit code templates.
4. Test a real sign-in email.

Sources:
- [resend/resend-cli on GitHub](https://github.com/resend/resend-cli)
- [Resend CLI 2.0 announcement](https://resend.com/blog/resend-cli-2.md)

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=83 session=341787a6-fe69-4600-9ee3-b107e505a42b]
timestamp: 2026-10-30T16:55:41.277Z

**No, a `.vercel.app` link won't work as your email domain.** Resend needs you to add DNS records, and Vercel owns `vercel.app`, so you can't add them. You'd need your own domain or subdomain, even a cheap one, connected to both Vercel and Resend.

The link itself will work for the website and API once it's deployed, but three things need care:

| Part | On Vercel |
|---|---|
| Next.js site and API | Should work; the template is built for Vercel |
| Supabase database and sign-in | Should work; add the same env values in Vercel |
| Mobile app | Point `EXPO_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` at the Vercel link |
| **Captions (`yt-dlp`)** | **Likely fails** |
| **Long Gemini reads** | **May hit Vercel's time limit** |

**Captions:** `yt-dlp` and `uvx` aren't on Vercel's servers, and YouTube often blocks server addresses anyway. The app would fall back to Gemini, so videos would be limited to 30 minutes and be slower. A captions API or a small separate server would fix this later.

**Time limit:** the route is set to 300 seconds, but what's actually allowed depends on your Vercel plan. I haven't checked the current limits. Long reads could be cut off.

For the hackathon, you don't need any of this, because 8x said localhost is fine. If you want to deploy later, get a domain first, because it fixes the email limit too.

Yes, basically. Both just send the sign-in email, and Supabase is still in charge of sign-in itself. It creates the code, checks it and manages the account. Resend only replaced the delivery step.

| | Supabase's built-in email | Resend |
|---|---|---|
| Sends sign-in emails | Yes | Yes |
| Custom email template | **Not on the free plan** | **Yes**, which is how the six-digit code got in |
| Email limits | Very low, a few an hour | Higher |
| Without your own domain | Limited | Only your Resend account email |
| Meant for | Trying things out | Real use |

So we didn't add Resend for sending as such. We added it because the free built-in service wouldn't let us put the code in the email, and the app needs that code.

Yes, logs are being committed. All 4 session logs in `.claude-logs/` are on GitHub, and the last log commit went in about 35 minutes ago with PR #6.

**One file has newer changes that aren't committed yet:** `2026-10-30_21-22-33_...md`, which covers this current session. That's expected, for three reasons:

- **The hook only writes the file.** It updates the log after each message on your machine, but it doesn't commit.
- **Logs go in with each PR.** The project rule is to commit them separately, right before a push, so I've been doing that with each PR.
- **Direct pushes to `main` aren't allowed,** and every push runs paid CI, so a push just for a log isn't worth it. Applying the database and changing settings didn't need a commit, so nothing has gone up since PR #6.

The newest lines stay on your Mac until the next PR. Before the final deadline, make sure one last log commit goes in, so 8x sees the whole session. I'll include the log with the class screen PR, or I can open a small logs-only PR now if you'd rather not wait.

That error usually means a damaged download, or a clash with an Expo Go already on your phone. Installing it again from the official source should fix it.

**Try this, in order:**
1. **Delete** `Expo-Go-55.0.7.apk` from that screen.
2. **Uninstall any Expo Go** already on your phone.
3. **Install Expo Go from the Google Play Store.** It's the safest route and matches your phone. It should support SDK 55, but check that once it's installed.
4. **Open Expo Go** and scan the QR code from inside it.

If the Play Store version is too old or isn't available, get the APK from [expo.dev/go](https://expo.dev/go), not from another GitHub page. Your download came from github.com, and I can't tell whether it was an official Expo release. Avoid APKs from unofficial repos.

**If it still fails,** there are two other ways to see it on your phone:
- **In your phone's browser:** open `http://192.168.1.11:8081` in Chrome. This needs no install, and the screens should load, but API calls would fail until I allow your phone's address. It's a small local change, so tell me if you want it.
- **Android development build:** this is the proper fix (8X-27), but it needs Android Studio or the Android SDK on your Mac. It may also be needed anyway if Expo Go can't run the app's native modules.

**To try it:** tap **I already have an account**, enter `devvshayan@gmail.com`, and use the code from your email. Then paste a YouTube link on Home. Sign-in might have a problem, because that address is plain `http`, which some browser security features don't allow. If it fails, send me a screenshot.

**About the Expo Go problem:** store Expo Go runs only SDK 58, and your app is on SDK 55. You have three ways forward, and I'd stick with the first for now.

| Option | Effort | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Phone browser** | Done | Fine for checking screens, but not a real app |
| **Android development build** | Android Studio or SDK install, then a build | The right fix (8X-27), and what your demo should use |
| **Expo Go for SDK 55** | Find an older build on [expo.dev/go](https://expo.dev/go) | Your first APK failed, and native modules may still break it |

An upgrade to SDK 58 isn't worth it during the hackathon, because the template is on 55.

**What I changed:** the local API now accepts calls from any address, in development only. That's uncommitted and goes in with the next PR. `expo-qr.png` is still in the repo folder, so I'll delete it next.

Yes, I've seen it: Arif merged [PR #7](https://github.com/devv-shayan/8x-hackathon/pull/7) about 5 minutes ago, and all 4 CI checks passed. Mostly good news, but the wireframes still use the old flow.

**What's in it**
- **Windows fixes:** the push checks now work on his machine, so he's clearly up and running. Those changes also touch the commit, secrets and standards check scripts.
- **Expo update:** `expo`, `expo-dev-client` and `expo-updates` moved to a newer SDK 55 patch, so the app is still on 55.
- **Mobile wireframes:** 12 phone screens and an index page in `wireframes-screenshots/mobile/`. The UI design is good.

**The problem: the wireframes are the old flow at phone size.** I viewed the index and three of the screens, and his summary says "see gaps, teach one student, explain again, one check, recap".

| Screen | Issue | Brief says |
|---|---|---|
| S6 Gaps, before teaching | Shows the right answers **before** the student asks about them | Keep gaps hidden until the class ends |
| S9 One check | Multiple choice | A student asks one question, answered in your own words |
| S8 Explain again | Still there, going by the index | Cut |
| S4 Source ready | "Preview, then hide it", so check it doesn't show the transcript | Title, thumbnail and length only |
| S10 Recap | Index says "What stuck, what's left" | Merged with gaps as "6 of 8" results |
| Warm-up, Topic page | Missing | Needed |

Two things are fine: S1 sign-in is right now that sign-in is on, and S7b works well as its own phone screen with "restate in your own words". I'd keep that design rather than forcing a bottom sheet, as long as it's quick to get back to the student.

**This is more than a design detail.** S6 before teaching and S9 multiple choice go against two core decisions in the brief, and 8X-28 lists both. He may not have read the full list. You two need to settle this before he builds those screens, especially as the transcript shows he hadn't finished reading the brief.

**Message you can send him:**

> Bhai wireframes acha lag raha hai, Windows fixes bhi zabardast. Bas 8X-28 check kar lena: gaps class ke baad aayen (S6 before teach na ho), S9 MCQ ki jagah student ka ek open question, S8 hata dein, aur warm-up + topic screens add karni hain. Pehle brief.md aur context.md parh lo, phir 10 min call karke final kar lete hain.

**On your side:** your local `main` is 10 commits behind, so you'll need a `git pull` and `npm install` before the class screen. Your only other changes are the uncommitted API setting, the session log and `expo-qr.png`.

This transcript changes our priorities. The biggest thing Maham said was: **move faster and send demos now.**

**What she said**
- **Speed:** Cohort 1 had working demos two days in, and she expects a product in 2–3 days.
- **Demos:** send a Loom after **each feature**, under 1 minute, camera on, talking through it. She wants updates over the weekend too, not just on Monday.
- **Order:** get the functionality working first, then auth, then UI.
- **Don't wait on each other:** build, and fix disagreements afterwards.
- **After the basics:** she'll judge initiative, meaning what creative or technically ambitious things you add.
- **Mobile:** start with the web app inside the monorepo, and add mobile later if you need it.

**What this changes**

1. **I got the mobile decision a bit wrong.** She didn't say to go mobile. She said web first, with the monorepo so mobile stays possible. The migration still fits, and the Expo app already runs in a browser, so nothing needs undoing. But **Android (8X-27) can drop down the list**, and your Loom can be recorded in the browser.
2. **Send a Loom today.** You haven't sent one yet, and paste link → key concepts with timestamps already works. That's a demo.
3. **Stop on sign-in and email work.** It's good enough for now, and she told you to leave auth until later. For Arif, local Supabase works today.
4. **Arif should build now.** Scoring (8X-15) doesn't need the wireframes finalised, and he shouldn't wait for you to review them.

**Your pitch doesn't match the brief.** On the call you described a second explanation at the end, scored against the first, but the brief cut that. Decide which you want and stick to it. I'd keep it cut, because comparing explanations minutes apart only measures short-term recall.

**New order for you**
1. Record a Loom of the current app, today.
2. Build the class (8X-11) and help panel (8X-12), sending a Loom after each.
3. Once the loop works, pick your "crazy thing". Students with real memory across sessions is your strongest option, and voice is the next.

Want me to write a short script for today's Loom, or start the class backend?

No. There are two fixed students, and their personas are written in the code, not generated by AI. They're created the first time someone teaches, and the same two appear in every class for that person.

| Student | Persona |
|---|---|
| **Maya** | A curious beginner who asks for plain explanations and everyday examples |
| **Omar** | A sharp critical thinker who questions reasons, edge cases and gaps in logic |

The AI writes each question in that student's voice, but it doesn't invent the personality. The code decides whose turn it is. The personas are in `DEFAULT_STUDENTS` in `apps/web/src/server/classroom/student-turn.ts`.

**Fixed personas suit your idea for now**, because "students who remember you" needs the same students coming back each time. If the AI made new ones per video, that continuity would be lost.

**If you want something more impressive for the out-of-the-box feature,** the better option is letting each student grow over time. Maya could keep a short memory of what you've taught her and what you got wrong, then refer to it in later classes. That's more original than generated personas, and it makes your main difference real.

It's fixed right now, at **4 questions per class: 2 from Maya and 2 from Omar, taking turns**. Your answers, the video's length and how many mistakes you made don't change that. A 4-minute video and a 90-minute lecture both get 4 questions.

Your answers do affect *what* gets asked, because the AI sees the class so far. They just don't affect how many, and a student can't follow up on a weak answer yet.

That was my last fix: I removed the AI's ability to stop early, because it was ending classes after 2 questions.

**I'd keep the 4-question limit, but make the questions smarter:**

| Change | Effect |
|---|---|
| **Follow-ups on weak answers** | If you skip or give a vague answer, the same student asks again more simply, instead of moving on |
| **Length based on what you got wrong** | One mistake might need 2 questions, and five might need 4 or 5 |
| **Ending when you're done** | A strong explanation gets a shorter class, but the app controls this, not the AI, so it can't stop early by accident |

The second and third need Arif's scoring (8X-15), because the app has to know how many concepts you missed. Follow-ups can be built now, and they'd make the class feel more like real students listening.

Keep a limit of around 4–5 either way. The brief promises about 10 minutes, and classes that run too long won't fit a daily habit.

Want me to add follow-ups now, or open the PR first?
